Not really, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like stress and aggravation with myself than sadness, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious all the time in the way I think of anxiety, like panic or this constant fear something terrible is going to happen.\n\nI do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, especially around work stuff, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just me getting wound up because I’m trying not to make another mistake. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, don’t miss this, don’t forget that,” and then I can get tense about it. But “on edge” as a general mood? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the thing that’s been most obvious is not always that I’m sitting there unable to focus, but that my concentration seems to drop out at exactly the wrong moments. Like I’ll be doing something very routine but important at work, and I know the steps, I know what I’m supposed to be looking for, and then I still miss something obvious.\n\nThat’s kind of what pushed me to come in. I keep making these stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make—like labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and realizing the wrong lower-third was still in there. And the really frustrating part is that sometimes I swear I checked it. I’ll look back and think, “I did review that,” but somehow my brain just didn’t actually catch it.\n\nIt makes me feel like I can’t trust my own attention, if that makes sense. Like I’m physically there, I’m trying to be careful, but something slips past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear change, honestly. My sleep isn’t something I’ve been tracking very closely. I mean, I have nights where I’m up later than I meant to be, or I’m still kind of wound up from the day and don’t settle down right away, but I don’t know if that’s new or unusual for me.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s been some dramatic shift, like suddenly I can’t sleep at all or I’m sleeping all day. At least, not that I’ve really noticed. It’s more that if I’m thinking about work or replaying something I messed up, it can take me a bit to actually relax, but I don’t know if I’d call that a sleep problem exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting things that you would normally remember?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, exactly. Like, I can’t think of a bunch of examples where I’m forgetting appointments or people’s names or something I’d normally be really solid on.\n\nWhat’s confusing is that sometimes it feels less like “forgetting” and more like something just doesn’t stay in my head long enough if I don’t write it down or if there are too many steps happening at once. But I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting. I’ll have this moment of, “Wait, what was I about to do?” and then it comes back, or I retrace what I was doing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say my memory has changed in some obvious way, at least not that I’ve been paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty enjoying activities or spending time with friends and family because of your concerns about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I definitely think about the mistakes afterward, probably more than I should, and it can make me feel tense or embarrassed. But I don’t know that I’ve stopped enjoying people or things because of it.\n\nLike if I’m with friends or family, I can still have a good time once I’m there. Sometimes work stuff is kind of in the back of my mind, especially if something went wrong that day, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or that I can’t enjoy them. It’s more like I might be a little distracted or preoccupied for a bit, and then I come around.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It bothers me, but I don’t think it’s really keeping me from enjoying activities or spending time with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very hard on yourself when you make one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m always extremely hard on myself, like I’m tearing myself apart every single time. But if I make a mistake, especially if I feel like it was avoidable, I do tend to replay it in my head for a while.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “I should’ve caught that,” and I can get pretty annoyed with myself. But I’m not sure if that’s more than what most people do. In my line of work, little mistakes can feel really noticeable, so it’s hard for me to tell whether I’m being unusually harsh or just… responding to the pressure of the situation.\n\nI don’t think I’d say it takes over my whole day every time, but yes, I can be critical of myself. I guess I just don’t know if I’d call it “very hard” or just frustrated with myself when I mess something up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that being critical of yourself after making a mistake tends to last for a while after the mistake has happened?\n\nPatient: It can, yeah, but I’m not sure how long “a while” is supposed to mean. Like, it’s not usually that I’m devastated for days or anything dramatic like that. But it can hang around longer than I’d like.\n\nIf I make a mistake, I might keep circling back to it later that day, especially during quiet moments—like when I’m driving home or trying to wind down. I’ll sort of replay what happened and think about what I should’ve done differently. Sometimes I can move on pretty quickly if it’s a small thing or if someone reassures me it wasn’t a big deal. But if it felt visible or like it affected someone else’s work, then it sticks more.\n\nI guess it’s more of an aftertaste than a constant thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always front and center, but it’ll pop back up and I’ll feel annoyed with myself again. So yes, it can last for a while, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or that it takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself still thinking about and criticizing yourself for mistakes even after a few days have passed?\n\nPatient: A few days later… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s as common for me. Like, if it was something really embarrassing or if I felt like it reflected badly on me, I might remember it again a couple days later and sort of wince about it, you know? But I don’t know that I’m still actively criticizing myself days afterward most of the time.\n\nUsually it’s more same-day, maybe into the next day if it was a bigger mistake. After a few days, unless something reminds me of it or there’s some consequence from it, it’s not usually taking up space in my head in the same way. I might think, “Ugh, that was dumb,” but it’s more like a quick flash than me sitting there beating myself up.\n\nSo… I guess not often, no. It happens occasionally, but I wouldn’t say it’s a regular thing after several days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after making one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure that’s exactly the word I’d use most of the time. I think I feel more embarrassed or irritated with myself than guilty, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nLike, if the mistake caused extra work for someone else or made someone else look bad, then yes, I can feel guilty about that. I’ll think, “I shouldn’t have put them in that position,” or “Now they have to deal with something because I missed it.” That part can bother me.\n\nBut if it’s just a mistake that’s contained to me, or something that gets fixed without much impact, it’s usually not guilt so much as frustration. I’ll feel stupid for missing something obvious, or I’ll worry that people noticed and are judging me. But guilt feels more like I did something wrong to someone, and I don’t know that I feel that every time.\n\nSo… sometimes, depending on the mistake and whether it affected someone else. But not automatically after every mistake, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your self-criticism after making a mistake affects your daily activities or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting my relationships in a clear way, like I don’t think people close to me would say, “Oh, she made a mistake and now she’s impossible to be around for days,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, if I’m still annoyed with myself, I might be a little quieter or distracted for a bit, but I don’t know that I’d connect that strongly to my daily life outside of the situation itself. Usually it feels more like something happening in my own head than something that spills out dramatically onto other people.\n\nDaily activities… maybe if I’m replaying something, it can take up mental space while I’m doing normal things, but I still do the things. I still eat dinner, answer messages, do whatever needs doing. I might not be as relaxed, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d say it really interferes.\n\nSo I’d say not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It can color my mood a little, but I don’t think it usually disrupts my life outside work or causes problems with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes and the stress they cause are significantly impacting your ability to function at work or enjoy your free time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work, yes. That’s where it feels like it’s actually starting to matter in a real way, not just me feeling bad in my own head.\n\nLike, the mistakes themselves don’t necessarily mean my camera work is bad — I know I’m good at that part, and I’ve had people say the actual footage is strong — but on more complicated shoots, where there are a lot of moving pieces and people need to trust that I’m tracking everything, I can tell some producers are less willing to hand me those responsibilities now. Or they’ll double-check me more, or give the more complex setup to someone else, and I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, they don’t fully trust me with this anymore.”\n\nAnd that’s been hard because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography kind of role, and it feels like that chance is slipping. Not because I can’t frame a shot or light something well, but because these mistakes and the stress around them make people hesitate with me. That’s the part that really gets to me.\n\nFree time… it probably affects my enjoyment some, mostly because I’ll carry that work stress home and keep thinking about what it means for my career. But the bigger impact is definitely work and feeling like I’m losing opportunities despite being capable of the actual creative side.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m avoiding them exactly. At least not in a clear, conscious way, like, “I’m not going to do that because I’ll mess it up.” It feels more complicated than that.\n\nIf someone asks me to do something, I usually still do it. I don’t think I’m turning things down or refusing certain setups. But I might hesitate more internally, if that makes sense. Like there’s a moment where I’m thinking, “Okay, don’t miss anything, don’t be the person who forgets some detail,” and that can make the task feel heavier than it probably should.\n\nBut actual avoidance… I’m not sure. It may be more that other people are becoming cautious about giving me certain responsibilities, rather than me actively avoiding them myself. I don’t think I’m hiding from work or trying to get out of things. I still want to do the more complicated work. I just feel more tense around it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your self-criticism about a mistake fades away within a day or so?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s always as clean as “within a day it’s gone.” Sometimes it does fade pretty quickly, especially if it was something small or if nothing really came of it. Like, if I realize later that nobody noticed or it didn’t cause an issue, then I can usually let it go faster.\n\nBut if it feels like the kind of mistake that makes me look unreliable, or if I think someone is going to remember it and factor it into whether they trust me, then it can linger more. Not necessarily in this dramatic way where I’m actively upset every minute, but it’ll sort of come back into my head. I’ll be making dinner or trying to relax and suddenly I’m replaying it again, thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “How did I miss that?”\n\nSo… within a day or so, sometimes, yes. But not always. It depends on how consequential it felt and whether I’m worried it changed how people see me.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to carry out your daily routines because of the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — mostly in the work sense. Like, if you mean daily routines as in brushing my teeth or getting groceries or basic stuff at home, I’m still doing those things. But my work routine, the way I’m supposed to move through a shoot and handle the responsibilities around it, that’s where it’s been getting harder.\n\nThe stress around making mistakes has started to change how people treat me on set, I think. Producers have started trusting me less on more complex shoots — not because the actual camera work is bad, because I do think that part is strong — but because when there are a lot of details and moving parts, they seem more hesitant to put me in charge of that. They’ll double-check me more, or give the more complicated pieces to someone else, and I can feel that shift.\n\nAnd that makes the routine harder because I’m not just doing the job anymore, I’m also thinking about whether I’m being watched, whether this is another moment where I might confirm their doubts. I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away even though I know I’m capable creatively. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.

The biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.

And the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.

I guess I just don’t know if I’d call it “very hard” or just frustrated with myself when I mess something up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that being critical of yourself after making a mistake tends to last for a while after the mistake has happened?\n\nPatient: It can, yeah, but I’m not sure how long “a while” is supposed to mean. That’s the part that really gets to me.\n\nFree time… it probably affects my enjoyment some, mostly because I’ll carry that work stress home and keep thinking about what it means for my career. But the bigger impact is definitely work and feeling like I’m losing opportunities despite being capable of the actual creative side.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m avoiding them exactly. At least not in a clear, conscious way, like, “I’m not going to do that because I’ll mess it up.” It feels more complicated than that.\n\nIf someone asks me to do something, I usually still do it. I don’t think I’m turning things down or refusing certain setups. But I might hesitate more internally, if that makes sense. Like there’s a moment where I’m thinking, “Okay, don’t miss anything, don’t be the person who forgets some detail,” and that can make the task feel heavier than it probably should.\n\nBut actual avoidance… I’m not sure. It may be more that other people are becoming cautious about giving me certain responsibilities, rather than me actively avoiding them myself. I don’t think I’m hiding from work or trying to get out of things. I still want to do the more complicated work. I just feel more tense around it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your self-criticism about a mistake fades away within a day or so?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s always as clean as “within a day it’s gone.” Sometimes it does fade pretty quickly, especially if it was something small or if nothing really came of it. Like, if I realize later that nobody noticed or it didn’t cause an issue, then I can usually let it go faster.\n\nBut if it feels like the kind of mistake that makes me look unreliable, or if I think someone is going to remember it and factor it into whether they trust me, then it can linger more. Not necessarily in this dramatic way where I’m actively upset every minute, but it’ll sort of come back into my head. I’ll be making dinner or trying to relax and suddenly I’m replaying it again, thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “How did I miss that?”\n\nSo… within a day or so, sometimes, yes. But not always. It depends on how consequential it felt and whether I’m worried it changed how people see me.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to carry out your daily routines because of the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — mostly in the work sense. Like, if you mean daily routines as in brushing my teeth or getting groceries or basic stuff at home, I’m still doing those things. But my work routine, the way I’m supposed to move through a shoot and handle the responsibilities around it, that’s where it’s been getting harder.\n\nThe stress around making mistakes has started to change how people treat me on set, I think. Producers have started trusting me less on more complex shoots — not because the actual camera work is bad, because I do think that part is strong — but because when there are a lot of details and moving parts, they seem more hesitant to put me in charge of that. They’ll double-check me more, or give the more complicated pieces to someone else, and I can feel that shift.\n\nAnd that makes the routine harder because I’m not just doing the job anymore, I’m also thinking about whether I’m being watched, whether this is another moment where I might confirm their doubts. I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away even though I know I’m capable creatively. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your overall well-being and daily life are being significantly affected by the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.\n\nThe biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely at work. I mean, I still interact with people and I can still do my job, but the dynamic has changed, and that’s been hard to ignore.

The biggest impact is with my producers. They’ve started trusting me less on complex shoots — or at least that’s how it feels from the way assignments and responsibilities are being handled. If it’s something straightforward, I’m usually fine, and I think they know the camera work itself is strong. Like, the footage, the framing, the actual visual side of what I do — I don’t think that’s the problem.

But when a shoot has a lot of moving parts, I can feel people hesitating. They’ll double-check things more, or they’ll steer the more complicated responsibilities toward someone else, and then I get in my head about it because I know they’re probably remembering past mistakes or missed details. So I’m trying to work, but I’m also managing this layer of stress about whether I’m being evaluated or whether I’m proving their concerns right.

And the really upsetting part is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Not because I don’t have the skill visually, but because people seem less confident that I can handle the complexity. So yes, it’s significantly affecting my work and how I relate to people there.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally scared me into coming in is that I’ve been making these… dumb technical mistakes at work that I absolutely know better than to make. I’m a camera operator and editor, so details matter, and I’ll do things like label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note, or export a client cut with the wrong lower-third still in it—even after I’ve checked it.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because these aren’t things I don’t understand. I know the workflow, I know what’s important, I know what can mess up a shoot or make a client lose confidence. It bothers me, but I don’t think it’s really keeping me from enjoying activities or spending time with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very hard on yourself when you make one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m always extremely hard on myself, like I’m tearing myself apart every single time. But if I make a mistake, especially if I feel like it was avoidable, I do tend to replay it in my head for a while.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “I should’ve caught that,” and I can get pretty annoyed with myself. But I’m not sure if that’s more than what most people do. In my line of work, little mistakes can feel really noticeable, so it’s hard for me to tell whether I’m being unusually harsh or just… responding to the pressure of the situation.\n\nI don’t think I’d say it takes over my whole day every time, but yes, I can be critical of myself. I guess I just don’t know if I’d call it “very hard” or just frustrated with myself when I mess something up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that being critical of yourself after making a mistake tends to last for a while after the mistake has happened?\n\nPatient: It can, yeah, but I’m not sure how long “a while” is supposed to mean. Like, it’s not usually that I’m devastated for days or anything dramatic like that. But it can hang around longer than I’d like.\n\nIf I make a mistake, I might keep circling back to it later that day, especially during quiet moments—like when I’m driving home or trying to wind down. I’ll sort of replay what happened and think about what I should’ve done differently. Sometimes I can move on pretty quickly if it’s a small thing or if someone reassures me it wasn’t a big deal. But if it felt visible or like it affected someone else’s work, then it sticks more.\n\nI guess it’s more of an aftertaste than a constant thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always front and center, but it’ll pop back up and I’ll feel annoyed with myself again. So yes, it can last for a while, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or that it takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself still thinking about and criticizing yourself for mistakes even after a few days have passed?\n\nPatient: A few days later… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s as common for me. Like, if it was something really embarrassing or if I felt like it reflected badly on me, I might remember it again a couple days later and sort of wince about it, you know? But I don’t know that I’m still actively criticizing myself days afterward most of the time.\n\nUsually it’s more same-day, maybe into the next day if it was a bigger mistake. After a few days, unless something reminds me of it or there’s some consequence from it, it’s not usually taking up space in my head in the same way. It happens occasionally, but I wouldn’t say it’s a regular thing after several days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after making one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure that’s exactly the word I’d use most of the time. I’ll feel stupid for missing something obvious, or I’ll worry that people noticed and are judging me. Usually it feels more like something happening in my own head than something that spills out dramatically onto other people.\n\nDaily activities… maybe if I’m replaying something, it can take up mental space while I’m doing normal things, but I still do the things. I still eat dinner, answer messages, do whatever needs doing. I might not be as relaxed, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d say it really interferes.\n\nSo I’d say not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It can color my mood a little, but I don’t think it usually disrupts my life outside work or causes problems with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes and the stress they cause are significantly impacting your ability to function at work or enjoy your free time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work, yes. That’s where it feels like it’s actually starting to matter in a real way, not just me feeling bad in my own head.\n\nLike, the mistakes themselves don’t necessarily mean my camera work is bad — I know I’m good at that part, and I’ve had people say the actual footage is strong — but on more complicated shoots, where there are a lot of moving pieces and people need to trust that I’m tracking everything, I can tell some producers are less willing to hand me those responsibilities now. Or they’ll double-check me more, or give the more complex setup to someone else, and I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, they don’t fully trust me with this anymore.”\n\nAnd that’s been hard because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography kind of role, and it feels like that chance is slipping. Not because I can’t frame a shot or light something well, but because these mistakes and the stress around them make people hesitate with me. That’s the part that really gets to me.\n\nFree time… it probably affects my enjoyment some, mostly because I’ll carry that work stress home and keep thinking about what it means for my career. But the bigger impact is definitely work and feeling like I’m losing opportunities despite being capable of the actual creative side.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m avoiding them exactly. At least not in a clear, conscious way, like, “I’m not going to do that because I’ll mess it up.” It feels more complicated than that.\n\nIf someone asks me to do something, I usually still do it. I don’t think I’m turning things down or refusing certain setups. But I might hesitate more internally, if that makes sense. Like there’s a moment where I’m thinking, “Okay, don’t miss anything, don’t be the person who forgets some detail,” and that can make the task feel heavier than it probably should.\n\nBut actual avoidance… I’m not sure. It may be more that other people are becoming cautious about giving me certain responsibilities, rather than me actively avoiding them myself. I don’t think I’m hiding from work or trying to get out of things. I still want to do the more complicated work. I just feel more tense around it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your self-criticism about a mistake fades away within a day or so?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s always as clean as “within a day it’s gone.” Sometimes it does fade pretty quickly, especially if it was something small or if nothing really came of it. Like, if I realize later that nobody noticed or it didn’t cause an issue, then I can usually let it go faster.\n\nBut if it feels like the kind of mistake that makes me look unreliable, or if I think someone is going to remember it and factor it into whether they trust me, then it can linger more. Not necessarily in this dramatic way where I’m actively upset every minute, but it’ll sort of come back into my head. I’ll be making dinner or trying to relax and suddenly I’m replaying it again, thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “How did I miss that?”\n\nSo… within a day or so, sometimes, yes. But not always. It depends on how consequential it felt and whether I’m worried it changed how people see me.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to carry out your daily routines because of the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — mostly in the work sense. Like, if you mean daily routines as in brushing my teeth or getting groceries or basic stuff at home, I’m still doing those things. But my work routine, the way I’m supposed to move through a shoot and handle the responsibilities around it, that’s where it’s been getting harder.\n\nThe stress around making mistakes has started to change how people treat me on set, I think. Producers have started trusting me less on more complex shoots — not because the actual camera work is bad, because I do think that part is strong — but because when there are a lot of details and moving parts, they seem more hesitant to put me in charge of that. They’ll double-check me more, or give the more complicated pieces to someone else, and I can feel that shift.\n\nAnd that makes the routine harder because I’m not just doing the job anymore, I’m also thinking about whether I’m being watched, whether this is another moment where I might confirm their doubts. I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away even though I know I’m capable creatively. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your overall well-being and daily life are being significantly affected by the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.\n\nThe biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to work or interact with others is being significantly impacted by your concerns about making mistakes and the stress that follows?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at work. I mean, I still interact with people and I can still do my job, but the dynamic has changed, and that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nThe biggest impact is with my producers. They’ve started trusting me less on complex shoots — or at least that’s how it feels from the way assignments and responsibilities are being handled. If it’s something straightforward, I’m usually fine, and I think they know the camera work itself is strong. Like, the footage, the framing, the actual visual side of what I do — I don’t think that’s the problem.\n\nBut when a shoot has a lot of moving parts, I can feel people hesitating. They’ll double-check things more, or they’ll steer the more complicated responsibilities toward someone else, and then I get in my head about it because I know they’re probably remembering past mistakes or missed details. So I’m trying to work, but I’m also managing this layer of stress about whether I’m being evaluated or whether I’m proving their concerns right.\n\nAnd the really upsetting part is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Not because I don’t have the skill visually, but because people seem less confident that I can handle the complexity. So yes, it’s significantly affecting my work and how I relate to people there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your overall well-being and daily life are being significantly affected by the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.\n\nThe biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to work or interact with others is being significantly impacted by your concerns about making mistakes and the stress that follows?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at work. I mean, I still interact with people and I can still do my job, but the dynamic has changed, and that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nThe biggest impact is with my producers. They’ve started trusting me less on complex shoots — or at least that’s how it feels from the way assignments and responsibilities are being handled. If it’s something straightforward, I’m usually fine, and I think they know the camera work itself is strong. Like, the footage, the framing, the actual visual side of what I do — I don’t think that’s the problem.\n\nBut when a shoot has a lot of moving parts, I can feel people hesitating. They’ll double-check things more, or they’ll steer the more complicated responsibilities toward someone else, and then I get in my head about it because I know they’re probably remembering past mistakes or missed details. So I’m trying to work, but I’m also managing this layer of stress about whether I’m being evaluated or whether I’m proving their concerns right.\n\nAnd the really upsetting part is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Not because I don’t have the skill visually, but because people seem less confident that I can handle the complexity. So yes, it’s significantly affecting my work and how I relate to people there.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you finding that your concerns about making mistakes and the stress they cause are affecting your personal relationships or daily activities outside of your job?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 38 / television_video_or_film_camera_operator_or_editor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work… I’m not really sure it’s affecting things in the same way. I mean, I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed some big impact on my personal relationships or my normal daily stuff at home.

Like, I still see people, I still handle errands, I’m not avoiding friends because of it or anything obvious like that. Most of the stress is tied to the job and the feeling that mistakes there are changing how people see me professionally. Once I’m away from that environment, it’s not like it completely disappears from my mind, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s causing major problems in my relationships or daily activities outside of work.

I guess if anything, I might be a little preoccupied sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example of it damaging things outside work. It’s really the work piece that feels like the main issue.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally scared me into coming in is that I’ve been making these… dumb technical mistakes at work that I absolutely know better than to make. I’m a camera operator and editor, so details matter, and I’ll do things like label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note, or export a client cut with the wrong lower-third still in it—even after I’ve checked it.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because these aren’t things I don’t understand. I know the workflow, I know what’s important, I know what can mess up a shoot or make a client lose confidence. But somehow I keep missing these things, and then afterward I’m like, “How did I not catch that?” It’s been happening enough that I started feeling really worried, like this isn’t just a one-off stressful day kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely been upset and frustrated with myself, and I get kind of embarrassed when something slips through at work, because it feels like I should be able to control it better.\n\nBut sad or down like… waking up feeling hopeless or crying all the time? Not really, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like stress and aggravation with myself than sadness, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I feel anxious all the time in the way I think of anxiety, like panic or this constant fear something terrible is going to happen.\n\nI do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, especially around work stuff, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just me getting wound up because I’m trying not to make another mistake. Like I’ll be thinking, “Okay, don’t miss this, don’t forget that,” and then I can get tense about it. But “on edge” as a general mood? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the thing that’s been most obvious is not always that I’m sitting there unable to focus, but that my concentration seems to drop out at exactly the wrong moments. Like I’ll be doing something very routine but important at work, and I know the steps, I know what I’m supposed to be looking for, and then I still miss something obvious.\n\nThat’s kind of what pushed me to come in. I keep making these stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make—like labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and realizing the wrong lower-third was still in there. And the really frustrating part is that sometimes I swear I checked it. I’ll look back and think, “I did review that,” but somehow my brain just didn’t actually catch it.\n\nIt makes me feel like I can’t trust my own attention, if that makes sense. Like I’m physically there, I’m trying to be careful, but something slips past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear change, honestly. My sleep isn’t something I’ve been tracking very closely. Sometimes work stuff is kind of in the back of my mind, especially if something went wrong that day, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or that I can’t enjoy them. It’s more like I might be a little distracted or preoccupied for a bit, and then I come around.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It bothers me, but I don’t think it’s really keeping me from enjoying activities or spending time with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very hard on yourself when you make one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m always extremely hard on myself, like I’m tearing myself apart every single time. But if I make a mistake, especially if I feel like it was avoidable, I do tend to replay it in my head for a while.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “I should’ve caught that,” and I can get pretty annoyed with myself. But I’m not sure if that’s more than what most people do. In my line of work, little mistakes can feel really noticeable, so it’s hard for me to tell whether I’m being unusually harsh or just… responding to the pressure of the situation.\n\nI don’t think I’d say it takes over my whole day every time, but yes, I can be critical of myself. I guess I just don’t know if I’d call it “very hard” or just frustrated with myself when I mess something up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that being critical of yourself after making a mistake tends to last for a while after the mistake has happened?\n\nPatient: It can, yeah, but I’m not sure how long “a while” is supposed to mean. Like, it’s not usually that I’m devastated for days or anything dramatic like that. But it can hang around longer than I’d like.\n\nIf I make a mistake, I might keep circling back to it later that day, especially during quiet moments—like when I’m driving home or trying to wind down. I’ll sort of replay what happened and think about what I should’ve done differently. Sometimes I can move on pretty quickly if it’s a small thing or if someone reassures me it wasn’t a big deal. But if it felt visible or like it affected someone else’s work, then it sticks more.\n\nI guess it’s more of an aftertaste than a constant thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always front and center, but it’ll pop back up and I’ll feel annoyed with myself again. So yes, it can last for a while, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or that it takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself still thinking about and criticizing yourself for mistakes even after a few days have passed?\n\nPatient: A few days later… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s as common for me. Like, if it was something really embarrassing or if I felt like it reflected badly on me, I might remember it again a couple days later and sort of wince about it, you know? But I don’t know that I’m still actively criticizing myself days afterward most of the time.\n\nUsually it’s more same-day, maybe into the next day if it was a bigger mistake. After a few days, unless something reminds me of it or there’s some consequence from it, it’s not usually taking up space in my head in the same way. I might think, “Ugh, that was dumb,” but it’s more like a quick flash than me sitting there beating myself up.\n\nSo… I guess not often, no. It happens occasionally, but I wouldn’t say it’s a regular thing after several days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after making one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure that’s exactly the word I’d use most of the time. I think I feel more embarrassed or irritated with myself than guilty, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nLike, if the mistake caused extra work for someone else or made someone else look bad, then yes, I can feel guilty about that. I’ll think, “I shouldn’t have put them in that position,” or “Now they have to deal with something because I missed it.” That part can bother me.\n\nBut if it’s just a mistake that’s contained to me, or something that gets fixed without much impact, it’s usually not guilt so much as frustration. I’ll feel stupid for missing something obvious, or I’ll worry that people noticed and are judging me. But guilt feels more like I did something wrong to someone, and I don’t know that I feel that every time.\n\nSo… sometimes, depending on the mistake and whether it affected someone else. But not automatically after every mistake, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your self-criticism after making a mistake affects your daily activities or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting my relationships in a clear way, like I don’t think people close to me would say, “Oh, she made a mistake and now she’s impossible to be around for days,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, if I’m still annoyed with myself, I might be a little quieter or distracted for a bit, but I don’t know that I’d connect that strongly to my daily life outside of the situation itself. Or they’ll double-check me more, or give the more complex setup to someone else, and I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, they don’t fully trust me with this anymore.”\n\nAnd that’s been hard because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography kind of role, and it feels like that chance is slipping. Not because I can’t frame a shot or light something well, but because these mistakes and the stress around them make people hesitate with me. That’s the part that really gets to me.\n\nFree time… it probably affects my enjoyment some, mostly because I’ll carry that work stress home and keep thinking about what it means for my career. But the bigger impact is definitely work and feeling like I’m losing opportunities despite being capable of the actual creative side.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m avoiding them exactly. At least not in a clear, conscious way, like, “I’m not going to do that because I’ll mess it up.” It feels more complicated than that.\n\nIf someone asks me to do something, I usually still do it. I don’t think I’m turning things down or refusing certain setups. But I might hesitate more internally, if that makes sense. Like there’s a moment where I’m thinking, “Okay, don’t miss anything, don’t be the person who forgets some detail,” and that can make the task feel heavier than it probably should.\n\nBut actual avoidance… I’m not sure. It may be more that other people are becoming cautious about giving me certain responsibilities, rather than me actively avoiding them myself. I don’t think I’m hiding from work or trying to get out of things. I still want to do the more complicated work. I just feel more tense around it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your self-criticism about a mistake fades away within a day or so?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s always as clean as “within a day it’s gone.” Sometimes it does fade pretty quickly, especially if it was something small or if nothing really came of it. Like, if I realize later that nobody noticed or it didn’t cause an issue, then I can usually let it go faster.\n\nBut if it feels like the kind of mistake that makes me look unreliable, or if I think someone is going to remember it and factor it into whether they trust me, then it can linger more. Not necessarily in this dramatic way where I’m actively upset every minute, but it’ll sort of come back into my head. I’ll be making dinner or trying to relax and suddenly I’m replaying it again, thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “How did I miss that?”\n\nSo… within a day or so, sometimes, yes. But not always. It depends on how consequential it felt and whether I’m worried it changed how people see me.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to carry out your daily routines because of the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — mostly in the work sense. Like, if you mean daily routines as in brushing my teeth or getting groceries or basic stuff at home, I’m still doing those things. But my work routine, the way I’m supposed to move through a shoot and handle the responsibilities around it, that’s where it’s been getting harder.\n\nThe stress around making mistakes has started to change how people treat me on set, I think. Producers have started trusting me less on more complex shoots — not because the actual camera work is bad, because I do think that part is strong — but because when there are a lot of details and moving parts, they seem more hesitant to put me in charge of that. They’ll double-check me more, or give the more complicated pieces to someone else, and I can feel that shift.\n\nAnd that makes the routine harder because I’m not just doing the job anymore, I’m also thinking about whether I’m being watched, whether this is another moment where I might confirm their doubts. I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away even though I know I’m capable creatively. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your overall well-being and daily life are being significantly affected by the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.\n\nThe biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to work or interact with others is being significantly impacted by your concerns about making mistakes and the stress that follows?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at work. I mean, I still interact with people and I can still do my job, but the dynamic has changed, and that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nThe biggest impact is with my producers. They’ve started trusting me less on complex shoots — or at least that’s how it feels from the way assignments and responsibilities are being handled. I mean, I have nights where I’m up later than I meant to be, or I’m still kind of wound up from the day and don’t settle down right away, but I don’t know if that’s new or unusual for me.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s been some dramatic shift, like suddenly I can’t sleep at all or I’m sleeping all day. At least, not that I’ve really noticed. It’s more that if I’m thinking about work or replaying something I messed up, it can take me a bit to actually relax, but I don’t know if I’d call that a sleep problem exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting things that you would normally remember?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, exactly. Like, I can’t think of a bunch of examples where I’m forgetting appointments or people’s names or something I’d normally be really solid on.\n\nWhat’s confusing is that sometimes it feels less like “forgetting” and more like something just doesn’t stay in my head long enough if I don’t write it down or if there are too many steps happening at once. But I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting. I’ll have this moment of, “Wait, what was I about to do?” and then it comes back, or I retrace what I was doing.\n\nSo… I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say my memory has changed in some obvious way, at least not that I’ve been paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty enjoying activities or spending time with friends and family because of your concerns about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I definitely think about the mistakes afterward, probably more than I should, and it can make me feel tense or embarrassed. But I don’t know that I’ve stopped enjoying people or things because of it.\n\nLike if I’m with friends or family, I can still have a good time once I’m there. Sometimes work stuff is kind of in the back of my mind, especially if something went wrong that day, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people or that I can’t enjoy them. It’s more like I might be a little distracted or preoccupied for a bit, and then I come around.\n\nSo… I guess no, not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It bothers me, but I don’t think it’s really keeping me from enjoying activities or spending time with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very hard on yourself when you make one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m always extremely hard on myself, like I’m tearing myself apart every single time. But if I make a mistake, especially if I feel like it was avoidable, I do tend to replay it in my head for a while.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” or “I should’ve caught that,” and I can get pretty annoyed with myself. But I’m not sure if that’s more than what most people do. In my line of work, little mistakes can feel really noticeable, so it’s hard for me to tell whether I’m being unusually harsh or just… responding to the pressure of the situation.\n\nI don’t think I’d say it takes over my whole day every time, but yes, I can be critical of myself. I guess I just don’t know if I’d call it “very hard” or just frustrated with myself when I mess something up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that being critical of yourself after making a mistake tends to last for a while after the mistake has happened?\n\nPatient: It can, yeah, but I’m not sure how long “a while” is supposed to mean. Like, it’s not usually that I’m devastated for days or anything dramatic like that. But it can hang around longer than I’d like.\n\nIf I make a mistake, I might keep circling back to it later that day, especially during quiet moments—like when I’m driving home or trying to wind down. I’ll sort of replay what happened and think about what I should’ve done differently. Sometimes I can move on pretty quickly if it’s a small thing or if someone reassures me it wasn’t a big deal. But if it felt visible or like it affected someone else’s work, then it sticks more.\n\nI guess it’s more of an aftertaste than a constant thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always front and center, but it’ll pop back up and I’ll feel annoyed with myself again. So yes, it can last for a while, but I don’t know that it’s extreme or that it takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself still thinking about and criticizing yourself for mistakes even after a few days have passed?\n\nPatient: A few days later… I’m not sure. I don’t think that’s as common for me. Like, if it was something really embarrassing or if I felt like it reflected badly on me, I might remember it again a couple days later and sort of wince about it, you know? But I don’t know that I’m still actively criticizing myself days afterward most of the time.\n\nUsually it’s more same-day, maybe into the next day if it was a bigger mistake. After a few days, unless something reminds me of it or there’s some consequence from it, it’s not usually taking up space in my head in the same way. I might think, “Ugh, that was dumb,” but it’s more like a quick flash than me sitting there beating myself up.\n\nSo… I guess not often, no. It happens occasionally, but I wouldn’t say it’s a regular thing after several days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after making one of these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I’m not sure that’s exactly the word I’d use most of the time. I think I feel more embarrassed or irritated with myself than guilty, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nLike, if the mistake caused extra work for someone else or made someone else look bad, then yes, I can feel guilty about that. I’ll think, “I shouldn’t have put them in that position,” or “Now they have to deal with something because I missed it.” That part can bother me.\n\nBut if it’s just a mistake that’s contained to me, or something that gets fixed without much impact, it’s usually not guilt so much as frustration. I’ll feel stupid for missing something obvious, or I’ll worry that people noticed and are judging me. But guilt feels more like I did something wrong to someone, and I don’t know that I feel that every time.\n\nSo… sometimes, depending on the mistake and whether it affected someone else. But not automatically after every mistake, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your self-criticism after making a mistake affects your daily activities or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it affecting my relationships in a clear way, like I don’t think people close to me would say, “Oh, she made a mistake and now she’s impossible to be around for days,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, if I’m still annoyed with myself, I might be a little quieter or distracted for a bit, but I don’t know that I’d connect that strongly to my daily life outside of the situation itself. Usually it feels more like something happening in my own head than something that spills out dramatically onto other people.\n\nDaily activities… maybe if I’m replaying something, it can take up mental space while I’m doing normal things, but I still do the things. I still eat dinner, answer messages, do whatever needs doing. I might not be as relaxed, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d say it really interferes.\n\nSo I’d say not in a major way that I’ve noticed. It can color my mood a little, but I don’t think it usually disrupts my life outside work or causes problems with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes and the stress they cause are significantly impacting your ability to function at work or enjoy your free time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work, yes. That’s where it feels like it’s actually starting to matter in a real way, not just me feeling bad in my own head.\n\nLike, the mistakes themselves don’t necessarily mean my camera work is bad — I know I’m good at that part, and I’ve had people say the actual footage is strong — but on more complicated shoots, where there are a lot of moving pieces and people need to trust that I’m tracking everything, I can tell some producers are less willing to hand me those responsibilities now. Or they’ll double-check me more, or give the more complex setup to someone else, and I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, they don’t fully trust me with this anymore.”\n\nAnd that’s been hard because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography kind of role, and it feels like that chance is slipping. Not because I can’t frame a shot or light something well, but because these mistakes and the stress around them make people hesitate with me. That’s the part that really gets to me.\n\nFree time… it probably affects my enjoyment some, mostly because I’ll carry that work stress home and keep thinking about what it means for my career. But the bigger impact is definitely work and feeling like I’m losing opportunities despite being capable of the actual creative side.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m avoiding them exactly. At least not in a clear, conscious way, like, “I’m not going to do that because I’ll mess it up.” It feels more complicated than that.\n\nIf someone asks me to do something, I usually still do it. I don’t think I’m turning things down or refusing certain setups. But I might hesitate more internally, if that makes sense. Like there’s a moment where I’m thinking, “Okay, don’t miss anything, don’t be the person who forgets some detail,” and that can make the task feel heavier than it probably should.\n\nBut actual avoidance… I’m not sure. It may be more that other people are becoming cautious about giving me certain responsibilities, rather than me actively avoiding them myself. I don’t think I’m hiding from work or trying to get out of things. I still want to do the more complicated work. I just feel more tense around it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find that your self-criticism about a mistake fades away within a day or so?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think it’s always as clean as “within a day it’s gone.” Sometimes it does fade pretty quickly, especially if it was something small or if nothing really came of it. Like, if I realize later that nobody noticed or it didn’t cause an issue, then I can usually let it go faster.\n\nBut if it feels like the kind of mistake that makes me look unreliable, or if I think someone is going to remember it and factor it into whether they trust me, then it can linger more. Not necessarily in this dramatic way where I’m actively upset every minute, but it’ll sort of come back into my head. I’ll be making dinner or trying to relax and suddenly I’m replaying it again, thinking, “Why did I do that?” or “How did I miss that?”\n\nSo… within a day or so, sometimes, yes. But not always. It depends on how consequential it felt and whether I’m worried it changed how people see me.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to carry out your daily routines because of the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so — mostly in the work sense. Like, if you mean daily routines as in brushing my teeth or getting groceries or basic stuff at home, I’m still doing those things. But my work routine, the way I’m supposed to move through a shoot and handle the responsibilities around it, that’s where it’s been getting harder.\n\nThe stress around making mistakes has started to change how people treat me on set, I think. Producers have started trusting me less on more complex shoots — not because the actual camera work is bad, because I do think that part is strong — but because when there are a lot of details and moving parts, they seem more hesitant to put me in charge of that. They’ll double-check me more, or give the more complicated pieces to someone else, and I can feel that shift.\n\nAnd that makes the routine harder because I’m not just doing the job anymore, I’m also thinking about whether I’m being watched, whether this is another moment where I might confirm their doubts. I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away even though I know I’m capable creatively. So yes, it’s affecting my ability to function day-to-day at work, because it’s changing what opportunities I’m getting and how confident people are in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your overall well-being and daily life are being significantly affected by the stress and mistakes you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I don’t want to make it sound like my entire life is falling apart, because it isn’t like I can’t get through a day or anything, but professionally it’s been affecting me in a pretty significant way.\n\nThe biggest thing is that my producers seem to trust me less on complex shoots now. And that feels awful, because the actual camera work — the creative part, the composition, lighting, framing, all of that — I feel like that’s still strong. That’s the part I know I’m good at. But when a shoot has a lot of moving pieces, I can tell they’re more hesitant with me. It’s like they’re thinking, “Can she keep track of everything?” And even if they don’t say it directly, I feel it in what they assign me or what they don’t assign me.\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that chance is slipping away. Not because I don’t have the eye for it or because my actual work looks bad, but because these mistakes or missed details make people question whether I can handle the bigger responsibility. So yes, my overall well-being is affected, because it’s not just stress in the moment — it feels like it’s changing my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to work or interact with others is being significantly impacted by your concerns about making mistakes and the stress that follows?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at work. I mean, I still interact with people and I can still do my job, but the dynamic has changed, and that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nThe biggest impact is with my producers. They’ve started trusting me less on complex shoots — or at least that’s how it feels from the way assignments and responsibilities are being handled. If it’s something straightforward, I’m usually fine, and I think they know the camera work itself is strong. Like, the footage, the framing, the actual visual side of what I do — I don’t think that’s the problem.\n\nBut when a shoot has a lot of moving parts, I can feel people hesitating. They’ll double-check things more, or they’ll steer the more complicated responsibilities toward someone else, and then I get in my head about it because I know they’re probably remembering past mistakes or missed details. So I’m trying to work, but I’m also managing this layer of stress about whether I’m being evaluated or whether I’m proving their concerns right.\n\nAnd the really upsetting part is that I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Not because I don’t have the skill visually, but because people seem less confident that I can handle the complexity. So yes, it’s significantly affecting my work and how I relate to people there.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that your concerns about making mistakes and the stress they cause are affecting your personal relationships or daily activities outside of your job?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not really sure it’s affecting things in the same way. I mean, I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed some big impact on my personal relationships or my normal daily stuff at home.\n\nLike, I still see people, I still handle errands, I’m not avoiding friends because of it or anything obvious like that. Most of the stress is tied to the job and the feeling that mistakes there are changing how people see me professionally. Once I’m away from that environment, it’s not like it completely disappears from my mind, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s causing major problems in my relationships or daily activities outside of work.\n\nI guess if anything, I might be a little preoccupied sometimes, but I don’t have a clear example of it damaging things outside work. It’s really the work piece that feels like the main issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s really the work piece that feels like the main issue.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're constantly 'on edge' and can't relax, even when you're not actively thinking about work or mistakes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 38 / television_video_or_film_camera_operator_or_editor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"Sitting still through a client screening feels like there’s pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up and do something with my body even when I know I’m supposed to just watch quietly.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so, but it’s a little hard for me to separate it from work because that’s where I notice it most clearly.

Like, a very specific example is client screenings. I’m supposed to just sit there and watch quietly, right? Just be present, take in the notes, be professional. But sitting still through those can feel almost physically uncomfortable. It’s like there’s this pressure building under my skin, like I need to get up or move or do something with my body, even if nothing is actually wrong in that moment.

And it’s not always that I’m consciously thinking, “Oh no, I made a mistake,” or “What if they’re judging me.” Sometimes I’m not having a big anxious thought at all. It’s more like my body is already activated. I’ll be sitting there trying to look calm and attentive, but internally I’m just… buzzing, I guess. Like I’m waiting for the screening to end so I can stand up, walk around, adjust something, do literally anything besides sit still.

So yes, I do feel on edge in that way. Not necessarily panicked, but restless — like relaxing doesn’t come naturally even when I know I’m supposed to just be still.
